                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901092 id="210901092" class="abstract-title js-splitscreen-abstract-title" >ABSTRACT</h2>
<section class="abstract" aria-label="Main abstract"><p class="chapter-para">New observations of the quasar HE0515â4414 have been made, aided by the Laser Frequency Comb (LFC), using the HARPS spectrograph on the ESO 3.6m telescope. We present three important advances for Î± measurements in quasar absorption spectra from these observations. First, the data have been wavelength calibrated using LFC and ThAr methods. The LFC wavelength calibration residuals are six times smaller than when using the standard ThAr calibration. We give a direct comparison between Î± measurements made using the two methods. Secondly, spectral modelling was performed using Artificial Intelligence (fully automated, all human bias eliminated), including a temperature parameter for each absorption component. Thirdly, in contrast to previous work, additional model parameters were assigned to measure Î± for each individual absorption component. The increase in statistical uncertainty from the larger number of model parameters is small and the method allows a substantial advantage; outliers that would otherwise contribute a significant systematic, possibly corrupting the entire measurement, are identified and removed, permitting a more robust overall result. The <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span><sub>abs</sub>Â = 1.15 absorption system along the HE0515â4414 sightline yields 40 new Î± measurements. We constrain spatial fluctuations in Î± to be ÎÎ±/Î± â¤ 9Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â5</sup> on scales <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\approx \!\! {20}\, {\rm km\, s}^{-1}$|â </span>, corresponding to <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\approx 25\,$|</span>kpc if the <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span><sub>abs</sub>Â = 1.15 system arises in a 1Mpc cluster. Collectively, the 40 measurements yield ÎÎ±/Î±Â = â0.27Â Â±Â 2.41Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup>, consistent with no variation.</p></section>                    <div class="article-metadata-panel clearfix at-ArticleMetadata"></div>
<div class="kwd-group"><a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;techniques: spectroscopic&quot;">techniques: spectroscopic</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;intergalactic medium&quot;">intergalactic medium</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;quasars: absorption lines&quot;">quasars: absorption lines</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;quasars: individual: HE0515â4414&quot;">quasars: individual: HE0515â4414</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;dark energy&quot;">dark energy</a>, <a class="kwd-part kwd-main" href="javascript:;" data-keyword="&quot;cosmology: observations&quot;">cosmology: observations</a></div>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901094 id="210901094" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec1>1 INTRODUCTION</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Fundamental constants, such as the fine structure constant (<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">â |$\alpha \equiv \frac{1}{4\pi \epsilon _0}\frac{e^2}{\hbar c}$|â </span>) and the proton-to-electron mass ratio (<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">â |$\mu \equiv \frac{m_p}{m_e}$|â </span>), are expected to vary in some modifications of General Relativity. A scalar field Ï coupling to the baryonic matter can produce temporal and/or spatial Î± variations (Bekenstein <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib14"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib14" data-open="bib14" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1982</a>; Sandvik, BarrowÂ &amp; Magueijo <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib83"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib83" data-open="bib83" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2002</a>; Copeland, NunesÂ &amp; Pospelov <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib25"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib25" data-open="bib25" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2004</a>; MarraÂ &amp; Rosati <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib58"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib58" data-open="bib58" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2005</a>; ShawÂ &amp; Barrow <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib87"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib87" data-open="bib87" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2005</a>; BarrowÂ &amp; Lip <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib12"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib12" data-open="bib12" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2012</a>). Î± may also vary with gravitational potential (Dicke <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib28"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib28" data-open="bib28" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1959</a>; Sandvik etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib83"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib83" data-open="bib83" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2002</a>; MotaÂ &amp; Barrow <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib65"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib65" data-open="bib65" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2004b</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib64"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib64" data-open="bib64" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">a</a>), or via interactions of baryonic matter with dark matter candidates (OliveÂ &amp; Pospelov <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib69"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib69" data-open="bib69" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2002</a>; StadnikÂ &amp; Flambaum <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib91"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib91" data-open="bib91" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2015</a>), or if the vacuum expectation value of Ï depends on the local density (Silva etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib88"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib88" data-open="bib88" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2014</a>). In theories with extra spatial dimensions (e.g. Kaluza-Klein and string theories), expansion (or contraction) of higher dimensions can produce observed changes to the coupling constants in our four-dimensional space time. Recent reviews of varying constants are given by Uzan (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib97"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib97" data-open="bib97" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2011</a>), Martins (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib59"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib59" data-open="bib59" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">Variations in Î± and Î¼ have been explored both on Earth through atomic clock measurements (Rosenband etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib80"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib80" data-open="bib80" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2008</a>), isotope ratio studies (DamourÂ &amp; Dyson <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib26"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib26" data-open="bib26" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1996</a>), and in space using astronomical observations of white dwarfs (Berengut etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib18"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib18" data-open="bib18" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>; Bainbridge etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib10"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib10" data-open="bib10" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>), galaxies (Bahcall, SteinhardtÂ &amp; Schlegel <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib9"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib9" data-open="bib9" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2004</a>), quasars (Webb etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib98"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib98" data-open="bib98" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1999</a>; Murphy, WebbÂ &amp; Flambaum <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib67"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib67" data-open="bib67" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2003</a>; Wilczynska etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib104"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib104" data-open="bib104" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2015</a>; Ubachs <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib95"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib95" data-open="bib95" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2018</a>), stars around the supermassive black hole in the Galaxy (Hees etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib40"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib40" data-open="bib40" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>), and the cosmic microwave background (Avelino etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib8"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib8" data-open="bib8" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2001</a>; Planck Collaboration <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib72"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib72" data-open="bib72" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2015</a>). A comprehensive analysis of 317 quasar absorption systems using the <em>Many Multiplet method</em> (Dzuba, FlambaumÂ &amp; Webb <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib33"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib33" data-open="bib33" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1999b</a>; Webb etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib98"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib98" data-open="bib98" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1999</a>) hinted at a spatial variation of Î±, modelled as a dipole with amplitude ÎÎ±/Î±Â = (Î±<sub>Q</sub> â Î±<sub>0</sub>)/Î±<sub>0</sub>Â = 1.1Â Â±Â 0.2Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup>, where Î±<sub>Q</sub> are quasar absorption measurements and Î±<sub>0</sub> is the terrestrial value (Webb etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib99"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib99" data-open="bib99" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2011</a>; King etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib44"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib44" data-open="bib44" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2012</a>; Wilczynska etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib103"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib103" data-open="bib103" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">Echelle spectrographs, using slit-based observations and calibrated using ThAr methods, are prone to long-range wavelength distortions (Molaro etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib63"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib63" data-open="bib63" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2008</a>; Rahmani etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib76"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib76" data-open="bib76" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>; Evans etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib35"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib35" data-open="bib35" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2014</a>). Such distortions, if present and left uncorrected, can significantly contribute to the total ÎÎ±/Î± measurement uncertainty (Evans etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib35"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib35" data-open="bib35" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2014</a>; KotuÅ¡, MurphyÂ &amp; Carswell <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib46"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib46" data-open="bib46" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>). Correction techniques include using additional external calibration information from asteroid observations (Molaro etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib62"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib62" data-open="bib62" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>; Rahmani etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib76"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib76" data-open="bib76" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>), iodine cells (Griest etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib37"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib37" data-open="bib37" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2010</a>; Whitmore, MurphyÂ &amp; Griest <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib102"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib102" data-open="bib102" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2010</a>), solar-twin observations (WhitmoreÂ &amp; Murphy <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib101"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib101" data-open="bib101" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2015</a>), or by using additional model parameters (DumontÂ &amp; Webb <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib29"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib29" data-open="bib29" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>). To a reasonable approximation, the best wavelength correction that could be achieved with any of these methods has an accuracy no better than <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\lesssim {30}\, {\rm m\, s}^{-1}$|â </span>. For comparison, the best laboratory accuracy of UV wavelengths used for ÎÎ±/Î± measurements is <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|${0.01}\, {\rm m\, s}^{-1}$|â </span>, three orders of magnitude better. Laser Frequency Comb (LFC; Udem, HolzwarthÂ &amp; HÃ¤nsch <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib96"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib96" data-open="bib96" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2002</a>; HÃ¤nsch <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib39"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib39" data-open="bib39" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2006</a>; Steinmetz etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib92"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib92" data-open="bib92" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2008</a>) wavelength calibration methods provide a vastly superior calibration than the correction methods above as they provide <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|${3}\, {\rm m\, s}^{-1}$|</span> accuracy for individual line centre measurements (MilakoviÄ etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib61"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib61" data-open="bib61" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>; Probst etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib74"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib74" data-open="bib74" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">In this paper, we report a set of high redshift Î± measurements from new observations of the quasar HE0515â4414. The observations (described in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec2"></span><a href="#sec2" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">2</a>) are of very high quality. These data are the first quasar spectral observations where the wavelength calibration has been carried out using an LFC. This means that any wavelength scale distortions present will be negligible. A second spectrum was produced from the same quasar observations but calibrated using ThAr methods. The two spectra enable a unique set of comparative tests to quantify uncertainties in searches for fundamental constant variations.</p><p class="chapter-para">We use new automated analysis methods (Lee etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib49"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib49" data-open="bib49" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>) to produce models for each spectrum and measure Î± using the Many Multiplet method (SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3"></span><a href="#sec3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3</a>). We introduce a new method, measuring Î± for each absorption component (rather than an average across an entire absorption complex). This provides considerably more detail and also offers a substantial advantage by enabling systematics to be more readily identified. We summarize our main findings in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec4"></span><a href="#sec4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">4</a> and discuss them in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec5"></span><a href="#sec5" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">5</a>.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901100 id="210901100" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec2>2 DATA</h2>
                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901101 id="210901101" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec2-1>2.1 Data acquisition</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">The spectrum used in this work was produced from high-resolution (<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">â |$R={\lambda }/{\Delta \lambda }=115\,000$|â </span>) observations using the High Accuracy Radial velocity Planet Searcher (HARPS) echelle spectrograph (Mayor etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib60"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib60" data-open="bib60" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2003</a>). HARPS is a double-channel echelle spectrograph built for extremely precise spectroscopic measurements. We observed HE0515â0414 (abbreviated as HE0515) between 2018 December 3 and 11 using HARPS in the classical fibre spectroscopy mode, where channel A recorded the HE0515 spectrum and channel B recorded the sky spectrum. We obtained 36 exposures totalling 52h 31m (TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl1" data-open="tbl1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">1</a>). Each exposure was bracketed by ThAr and LFC exposures for wavelength calibration. The sky was dark and the seeing conditions varied between 0.45 and 1.98 arcsec throughout the observing run. The median seeing (i.e. the median of column 3 in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl1" data-open="tbl1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">1</a>) is 1.34 arcsec. This has no influence on the final spectral resolution. The secondary guiding system ensures the object is consistently centred on the object image up to 0.01 arcsec and octagonal fibres ensure that the light evenly illuminates the spectrograph pupil. Therefore, telescope guiding and fibre illumination are not expected to introduce spectroscopic velocity shifts larger than 0.12âmâs<sup>â1</sup> (Lo Curto etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib53"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib53" data-open="bib53" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2015</a>).</p>                    <a id="210901103" scrollto-destination="210901103"></a>

class="chapter-para">Light entering the spectrograph is recorded on the detector (a mosaic of two EEV2k4 CCDs) for which the read-out mechanism is located on one of its sides (Mayor etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib60"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib60" data-open="bib60" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2003</a>; RodlerÂ &amp; Lo Curto <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib79"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib79" data-open="bib79" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2019</a>). By design, charge transfer occurs in the cross-dispersion direction to minimize effects of charge transfer inefficiency (CTI). Left uncorrected, CTI can introduce spurious spectroscopic velocity shifts up to 3âmâs<sup>â1</sup> for very low flux exposures (MilakoviÄ etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib61"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib61" data-open="bib61" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>). However, as no appropriate CTI model yet exists for HARPS, we do not correct for this effect.</p><p class="chapter-para">Raw images were reduced using the HARPS pipeline (version 3.8; RodlerÂ &amp; Lo Curto <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib79"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib79" data-open="bib79" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2019</a>). The pipeline extracts 1D spectra of individual echelle orders following optimal extraction by Horne (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib41"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib41" data-open="bib41" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1986</a>), Robertson (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib78"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib78" data-open="bib78" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1986</a>). Order tracing and pixel weights are determined from tungsten-lamp frames taken at the beginning of each night. Pipeline products previously demonstrated a 0.01âmâs<sup>â1</sup> precision (MilakoviÄ etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib61"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib61" data-open="bib61" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>), so we do not expect spectroscopic velocity shifts associated with its use.</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901106 id="210901106" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec2-2>2.2 Wavelength calibration and data addition</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">Wavelength calibration was obtained from LFC and ThAr frames taken immediately before each quasar exposure. The LFC has an offset frequency of 4.58âGHz and 18âGHz line separation. LFC wavelength calibration was performed using eight seventh-order polynomials per echelle order. Each echelle order spans eight 512-pixel blocks on the CCD (Wilken etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib105"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib105" data-open="bib105" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2010</a>; Molaro etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib62"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib62" data-open="bib62" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>). The accuracy of the LFC wavelength calibration is 3âmâs<sup>â1</sup>, measured by the root mean square (rms) of calibration residuals (i.e. known LFC line frequency minus the frequency determined from the wavelength solution at line position on the detector, see MilakoviÄ etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib61"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib61" data-open="bib61" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>). The ThAr wavelength calibration was produced by the HARPS pipeline using a single third-order polynomial per echelle order, with an accuracy of 27âmâs<sup>â1</sup>. The average difference in the two calibrations, considering wavelengths <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\lambda \geqslant {5000}\, {\rm \AA}$|â </span>, is â1.13âmâs<sup>â1</sup> (LFC minus ThAr).</p><p class="chapter-para">Comparing the true LFC line wavelengths to the ThAr-calibrated wavelengths at their location on the detector reveals a distortion pattern in the ThAr calibration, illustrated on Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig1"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig1" class="link xref-fig">1</a>. The pattern shows no long-range wavelength trends, but contains discontinuities associated with stitching of the HARPS detector (Wilken etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib105"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib105" data-open="bib105" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2010</a>; Molaro etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib62"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib62" data-open="bib62" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>), not accounted for by the pipeline calibration procedure. We discuss the impact of this distortion pattern on Î± measurements in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec5"></span><a href="#sec5" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">5</a>.</p>                    <a id="210901109" scrollto-destination="210901109"></a>
<div data-id="fig1" data-content-id="fig1" class="fig fig-section js-fig-section" swap-content-for-modal="true"><div class="graphic-wrap"><img class="content-image" src="https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/mnras/500/1/10.1093_mnras_staa3217/2/m_staa3217fig1.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&amp;Signature=4eGQi6KSNSvORGiMiRQfvpB5GWvmx2ezy5zqgt~g7tZWNtfa85BWkaSGr3I3Ana3KqQO9eTY0EC24ve0tUOr~AR5y5eUEpK9slsUzxp9RqfzGFS8MArmtcpjbuJn7JYdYZjsAbNcTt9TrWYVaV3PxCuS7neT3l1DLDDM19FkzJ6oufLx8rq71BGLMiWhMp7rANqAWqJ1I0UP0UWV7COzUGmTzKnLxQKAc2RmHT-QJkTUbyRtd3j94mwO57wbkBNjR69bbUmEghKMZiEIzJWZww0YXMGw8ZT91SJtEpHrqPHHW16ZV4cahyWaisxT5INiS0oXKyTfkccywSM5inLDLA__&amp;Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA" alt="Distortions in the ThAr calibration revealed by comparing with LFC lines. Each dot is a single LFC line. Distortion amplitude generally increases at order edges. Clear discontinuities associated with the HARPS detector stitching pattern (Wilken etÂ al. 2010; Molaro etÂ al. 2013) are seen for all orders. The distortions do not show long-range wavelength dependency, have a â1.1âmâsâ1 mean offset with respect to the LFC scale, and an overall scatter of 27.9âmâsâ1 rms. Panel (b) shows the region covered by echelle order 110 ($\lambda \approx {5500}\, {\rm \AA}$), also marked by a grey rectangle in panel (a)." data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig1.jpg" /><div class="graphic-bottom"><div class="label fig-label" id="label-210901109">Figure 1.</div><div class="caption fig-caption"><p class="chapter-para">Distortions in the ThAr calibration revealed by comparing with LFC lines. <div data-id="fig3" data-content-id="fig3" class="fig fig-section js-fig-section" swap-content-for-modal="true"><div class="graphic-wrap"><img class="content-image" src="https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/mnras/500/1/10.1093_mnras_staa3217/2/m_staa3217fig3.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&amp;Signature=Ye1z6pIkTLVoJJhHySgGkwglWeZIHy-PYb8uiT-3WKKgu392HohJgljg9x18ZsIMR7056RU5X8ggkskLPdKeiy76imD3zCs~xcEhNhaOwXZN0oUawQnZdL5uiRpnU1hoIhE215mpLPjDbOZ2PSmUlS89qubcnQQB-XIJ4UIqDZQFooFJ8BeXca4wx9SOXYrGjsmMXXdqQjaWxvRxWVK-wm1IilYsKF2Bo-RCcQ9CfuFXaQNkFwxBBQ7YCpk2gBBaLScjjb0NWYyjDBVHtcOp-BQ5NQevi7WrKrdk14AvRdIkfd5dPIIyKviuKMt~OQP8gEPj2OXieJ2iBxV9Z9gqKg__&amp;Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA" alt="Each histogram shows the average S/N per LFC line in a single LFC exposure as a function of echelle order. A single exposure (full red histogram) reaches S/N of â330 per LFC line and covers one additional echelle order (122). This exposure provides the most accurate wavelength calibration over the broadest wavelength range. Given the exquisite stability of HARPS (see Fig.Â 2), we choose to use this exposure to calibrate all quasar exposures." data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig3.jpg" /><div class="graphic-bottom"><div class="label fig-label" id="label-210901112">Figure 3.</div><div class="caption fig-caption"><p class="chapter-para">Each histogram shows the average S/N per LFC line in a single LFC exposure as a function of echelle order. Five exposures have significantly lower S/N than others (the reason is unknown). A single exposure (full red histogram) reaches S/N of â330 per LFC line and covers one additional echelle order (122). This exposure provides the most accurate wavelength calibration over the broadest wavelength range. Given the exquisite stability of HARPS (see Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig2"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig2" class="link xref-fig">2</a>), we choose to use this exposure to calibrate all quasar exposures.</p></div><div class="ajax-articleAbstract-exclude-regex fig-orig original-slide figure-button-wrap"><a class="fig-view-orig js-view-large at-figureViewLarge openInAnotherWindow" role="button" aria-describedby="label-210901112" href="/view-large/figure/210901112/staa3217fig3.jpg" data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig3.jpg" target="_blank">Open in new tab</a><a class="download-slide" role="button" aria-describedby="label-210901112" data-section="210901112" href="/DownloadFile/DownloadImage.aspx?image=https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/mnras/500/1/10.1093_mnras_staa3217/2/staa3217fig3.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&Signature=G-akDzZbsY287HPSFPZpLgLDOBU~yxcM22lPAJNcT6TYSX1JwdRzUH7LbfKXMwonjG-h5vGUlzZ6D-Z4aYmsadoVOjtHpPJ6-Xs7SFwGOTq1qxdEYAcBDaG1o3CGly6HtWA7~UbGhkHA~94eJYea18nqPvEKDvk2LoCcZ~H9zMbYl242Er9DNgdur1p3YUZ154XYl5ciXAOBTrOR0WZ-8fkIFWdrIuEGUneG5FxvezxUHIWVK9oWRh2Pcesu-1O0tv2wMsC68aRPZUYV1fEIh5qYp6efk0mEB8v7YDY~KlhDWwoL9TBEk7LtQsSKf33N22KsUJhfUuG-BKW9KZqQvQ__&Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA&sec=210901112&ar=5935255&xsltPath=~/UI/app/XSLT&imagename=&siteId=5326" data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig3.jpg">Download slide</a></div></div></div></div><p class="chapter-para">Although all LFC exposures were taken under the same nominal conditions and with same exposure times, it turned out that one exposure was substantially better (in terms of flux) than all others (Fig. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig3"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig3" class="link xref-fig">3</a>). Therefore, after careful consistency checking between multiple LFC exposures, this highest flux LFC exposure was used to wavelength calibrate all quasar exposures for wavelengths <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\lambda \geqslant {5000}\, {\rm \AA}$|</span> (the LFC data cuts off below this wavelength). There are no saturated LFC lines. We do not follow the same procedure for ThAr calibration, but calibrate each quasar exposure using the ThAr frame taken immediately beforehand.</p><p class="chapter-para">We transform the LFC and the ThAr wavelength scales to the Solar system barycentre rest frame using the barycentric velocity shift correction provided by the HARPS pipeline, independently for each quasar exposure. The barycentric correction provided by the pipeline is based on BretagnonÂ &amp; Francou (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib22"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib22" data-open="bib22" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1988</a>) and uses the flux-weighted average time of observation. This value agrees down to several mmâs<sup>â1</sup> with our independent calculation, using the same information and the <span class="monospace">astropy</span> module.<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fn2"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="fn2" data-open="fn2" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-fn js-xref-fn"><sup>2</sup></a></p><p class="chapter-para">Finally, we rebin the individual extracted spectra on to a common wavelength grid using a custom routine and sum them together, weighting each pixel by its error estimate (which includes the Poissonian error term, the read-out noise, and the dark current). The error array extracted during this procedure agrees with the estimate derived from flux rms over <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\approx {1}\, {\rm \AA}$|</span> range. The final co-added spectrum has an average signal-to-noise ratio (S/N) near 50 per 0.015âÃ pixel in the continuum. This data extraction process was performed for the LFC and ThAr calibration separately, producing two spectra from the same observations.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901116 id="210901116" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3>3 MODELLING PROCEDURE</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">The spectrum shows a damped Lyman-Î± absorption complex spanning <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|${700}\, {\rm km\, s}^{-1}$|â </span>, at redshift <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span><sub>abs</sub> â 1.15 (Reimers etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib77"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib77" data-open="bib77" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1998</a>), from which numerous previous measurements of Î± have been made (Quast, ReimersÂ &amp; Levshakov <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib75"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib75" data-open="bib75" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2004</a>; Levshakov etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib50"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib50" data-open="bib50" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2005</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib51"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib51" data-open="bib51" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2006</a>; Chand etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib24"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib24" data-open="bib24" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2006</a>; Molaro etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib63"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib63" data-open="bib63" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2008</a>; KotuÅ¡ etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib46"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib46" data-open="bib46" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>). There are at least 26 transitions useful for an Î± measurement in this system. The <em>Many Multiplet</em> analysis in this work makes use of transitions covered by the LFC calibration, listed in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tblA1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tblA1" data-open="tblA1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">A1</a>. None of the transitions we use blend with any other systems nor with transitions from the <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span>Â = 0.28 absorption complex identified by Bielby etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib19"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib19" data-open="bib19" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>). The LFC-calibrated spectrum showing the Mgâ<span class="small-caps">ii</span> Î»2796 transition is plotted as a black histogram in Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig4"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig4" class="link xref-fig">4</a>.</p>                    <a id="210901118" scrollto-destination="210901118"></a>

Thermal-only fits are marked by white diamonds. Turbulent-only fits are shown as white squares. The red shaded area marks the weighted average temperature, <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$T=9.12\pm 1.08\times 10^3\, {\rm K}$|â </span>. Individual measurements are scattered around this value with <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\chi ^2_\nu =1.042$|â </span>, where <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\nu$|</span>Â = 61. The panel to the right is the histogram of temperatures.</p></div><div class="ajax-articleAbstract-exclude-regex fig-orig original-slide figure-button-wrap"><a class="fig-view-orig js-view-large at-figureViewLarge openInAnotherWindow" role="button" aria-describedby="label-210901119" href="/view-large/figure/210901119/staa3217fig5.jpg" data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig5.jpg" target="_blank">Open in new tab</a><a class="download-slide" role="button" aria-describedby="label-210901119" data-section="210901119" href="/DownloadFile/DownloadImage.aspx?image=https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/mnras/500/1/10.1093_mnras_staa3217/2/staa3217fig5.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&Signature=gnFPVZBUa0PSzdqOvIY2N~EnXep8Hagy5vzreVk5~d2YWvyVCT~~E~lIHifUpwquUORIIxEHtH-kYA9J2vvli6otM3uOAKbzLJ78cn2TkYR18eEvOJIOM8HF0BsA60FLsvsMpkgMUGlD61IxLh0kCOm2l-4M7fIx8KGW5Lig8CRBqlO1N143mP0Wn2bcqcaDrMwJ1~Z2TKTNXDMLWF0rGqQHlVt6aEYXekAUdhT5HJID5uN0SNYhPlwTi2ZkSitkG7wiskYDHPD7ofxkd4q~Jwh2~u1m07dkOEuDuALbF16ZlWaxj7EOO4HbuhsYbTx0PDWgFBycepcG1nIpnayrHA__&Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA&sec=210901119&ar=5935255&xsltPath=~/UI/app/XSLT&imagename=&siteId=5326" data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig5.jpg">Download slide</a></div></div></div></div><p class="chapter-para">We use the most recent set of laboratory wavelength measurements, transition probabilities, oscillator strengths, and isotopic structures for the relevant transitions. These are given in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tblA1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tblA1" data-open="tblA1" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">A1</a>. The isotopic abundances were assumed to be solar (Asplund etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib7"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib7" data-open="bib7" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2009</a>). The sensitivity coefficients that relate atomic line shifts to a change in Î± are from Dzuba, FlambaumÂ &amp; Webb (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib32"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib32" data-open="bib32" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1999a</a>), Dzuba etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib34"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib34" data-open="bib34" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2002</a>), DzubaÂ &amp; Flambaum (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib30"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib30" data-open="bib30" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2009</a>). All atomic data is provided as online supplementary material.</p><p class="chapter-para">We use a fully automated modelling procedure, <span class="monospace">AI-VPFIT</span>, to produce a model of the absorption system (Lee etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib49"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib49" data-open="bib49" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>). <span class="monospace">AI-VPFIT</span>Â is a development of the approach introduced in BainbridgeÂ &amp; Webb (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib11"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib11" data-open="bib11" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>) and Bainbridge etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib10"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib10" data-open="bib10" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>). Model complexity is increased by placing absorption components (âtrial linesâ) at a random location in the velocity structure and checking if the newly introduced parameters are justified by the data. For the analysis described in the present paper, the optimal number of model parameters are derived using the corrected Akaike Information Criterion (Akaike <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib1"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib1" data-open="bib1" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1974</a>; Sugiura <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib93"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib93" data-open="bib93" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1978</a>). Performance tests using simulated data are described in Lee etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib49"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib49" data-open="bib49" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>). Redshifts and <em>b</em>-parameters of components appearing in multiple species are tied during fitting (SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-2"></span><a href="#sec3-2" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.2</a>). Column densities are free parameters. We include additional parameters for the unabsorbed continuum level for all transitions and for zero-level adjustment for saturated ones. Î± is also kept as a free parameter but this has been treated in two different ways (see SectionsÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a> andÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-4"></span><a href="#sec3-4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.4</a>). The basis of the AI algorithm is a genetic process in which a model is built up in 6 well-defined stages. An initial model for the absorption system is generated using a âprimaryâ species, that is, one atomic transition (or atomic species), selected to maximize line strength but avoiding line saturation. Subsequent stages incorporate further atomic species, with appropriately tied parameters, refine parameter errors, check for overfitting, and allow for âinterlopersâ, i.e. unidentified lines from other redshift systems that are needed to derive a statistically acceptable overall model.</p><p class="chapter-para">We produce models for the LFC-calibrated and ThAr-calibrated spectrum independently. All relevant settings during <span class="monospace">AI-VPFIT</span>Â modelling are kept the same (such as the number of attempts <span class="monospace">AI-VPFIT</span>Â will make to increase model complexity before proceeding to the following stage, default parameter values for trial lines, line dropping criteria, finite derivative step sizes, etc.), ensuring that all the differences between the final models are a direct consequence of differences in the input data. We refer to models produced from the LFC- and the ThAr-calibrated spectrum as the LFC and the ThAr models, respectively. Figures showing the data, the models, and the residuals for all transitions and all regions are in AppendicesÂ Â <a class="js-jumplink" href="#sec7">B</a> (for LFC) andÂ Â <a class="js-jumplink" href="#sec8">C</a> (for ThAr).</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901123 id="210901123" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3-1>3.1 Instrumental profile</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">The nominal HARPS instrumental profile has a full width at half-maximum of 2.61Â km s<sup>â1</sup>. The average <em>b</em>-parameter for individual absorption components in the absorption system analysed in this paper is â5Â km s<sup>â1</sup>. The observed quasar lines are thus well-resolved. When matching models to the observed data we must convolve Voigt profiles with the HARPS instrumental profile (IP). To do this we used a Gaussian IP. However, slight departures from a Gaussian have been reported. Moreover, these are found to vary with both flux and position on the detector MilakoviÄ etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib61"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib61" data-open="bib61" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>). A numerical profile determined directly from HARPS calibration data would thus provide a slightly more accurate IP. However, this was not possible due to insufficient available data.<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fn3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="fn3" data-open="fn3" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-fn js-xref-fn"><sup>3</sup></a></p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901125 id="210901125" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3-2>3.2 Temperature as a free parameter</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">The choice of a line broadening mechanism heavily influences the final model. We found that using turbulent broadening (i.e. not including temperature as a free parameter) impacts significantly on the analysis. For example, imposing a turbulent model forces <em>b</em> to be the same for all species irrespective of atomic mass. If turbulent broadening does not apply in practice, the consequence of the assumption is that additional velocity components are unavoidably included in order to achieve a satisfactory fit to the data. The converse is true - i.e. if a pure thermal model is imposed in the modelling procedure, additional velocity components may also be required to compensate if the model is inappropriate. We explored this by computing models for all three cases, i.e. turbulent, thermal, and mixed-<em>b</em>.</p><div class="&#xA;            block-child-p&#xA;            ">In the mixed-<em>b</em> model, the total line <em>b</em>-parameter is <div class="formula-wrap"><div class="disp-formula" id="jumplink-equ1" content-id="equ1"><div class="tex-math display-math">$$\begin{eqnarray*}
b^2 = b_{\rm turb}^2 + \frac{2kT}{m},
\end{eqnarray*}$$</div></div><span class="label title-label">(1)</span></div>where the right-hand-side terms are the turbulent and thermal contributions, respectively. In the thermal contribution, <em>k</em> is the Boltzmannâs constant, <em>T</em> is gas temperature, and <em>m</em> is the appropriate atomic mass. The contribution of each broadening mechanism is determined by the relative widths of transitions of different atomic masses.</div><p class="chapter-para">The interesting outcome was that a mixed-<em>b</em> model generally requires fewer components and also avoids spurious double-components in line centres (see Lee etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib49"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib49" data-open="bib49" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>). Further, once mixed-<em>b</em> models have been derived, it becomes apparent that temperature parameters are genuinely required by the data. The weighted average temperature is <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$T=9.12\pm 1.08\times 10^3\, {\rm K}$|â </span>. Individual temperature measurements are shown in Fig. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig5"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig5" class="link xref-fig">5</a>. The temperature is poorly estimated for some components (due to line blending and/or weak lines). Similar results are obtained for the ThAr-calibrated spectrum (not reported). The normalized Ï<sup>2</sup> of temperature measurements from 62 velocity components is 1.042, so the data appear to be consistent with a single temperature applying to all components.</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901129 id="210901129" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3-3>3.3 Subdividing the absorption complex â 5 regions</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">We initially divide the system into five regions, IâV (coloured regions in Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig4"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig4" class="link xref-fig">4</a>). Partitioning occurs where the normalized continuum recovers to unity. There is slight overlap between continuum regions in order to optimize continuum estimates. This partitioning has the benefit of simplifying computations and providing independent Î± measurements, whilst avoiding any potential bias that could occur if unidentified line blending corrupts part of the data.</p><p class="chapter-para">The five ÎÎ±/Î± measurements derived from splitting the absorption system into regions are tabulated in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl2"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl2" data-open="tbl2" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">2</a>. The quoted uncertainties are derived from the covariance matrix at the best solution. Other relevant statistical information, i.e. the number of free parameters for each region, the number of metal components and their average redshift, and the reduced Ï<sup>2</sup> of the model (<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">â |$\chi ^2_\nu =\chi ^2/\nu$|â </span>, where <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\nu$|</span> is the number of degree of freedom in the model), are also given. The weighted average of ÎÎ±/Î± measurements over all five regions for the LFC-calibrated spectrum is 0.94Â Â±Â 1.97Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup>. The same quantity for the ThAr-calibrated spectrum is 4.82Â Â±Â 1.92Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup>.</p>                    <a id="210901132" scrollto-destination="210901132"></a>

However, region II produces the seemingly anomalous result of ÎÎ±/Î±Â = 17.74Â Â±Â 4.30Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup> (i.e. a 4.1Ï deviation from zero). For the ThAr-calibrated spectrum, regions III, IV, and V are all consistent with ÎÎ±/Î±Â = 0. However, regions I and II are not. Region II produces a non-zero result that is similar to the LFC spectrum. Region I also gives a strongly positive result. We discuss ways in which such anomalies can arise in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-4"></span><a href="#sec3-4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.4</a>.</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901134 id="210901134" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3-4>3.4 Further subdivision â 47 measurements of Î±</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">Instead of dividing the complete absorption complex into five segments and obtaining five measurements of ÎÎ±/Î±, we can instead solve for the best-fitting model using one free ÎÎ±/Î± parameter for each individual absorption component in the complex. Doing so provides considerably more detail and can identify any ÎÎ±/Î± outliers that might âcorruptâ an Î± measurement derived over a whole region or complex. The cost is obviously that the number of free parameters is increased.</p><p class="chapter-para">To do this we accept the best-fitting models for each of the five regions and use these parameters as a starting point. However, additional parameters are included to allow Î± to vary independently for each velocity component. Optimization is done using <span class="monospace">VPFIT</span>. In other words, we do not recommence the entire <span class="monospace">AI-VPFIT</span>Â fitting process from scratch. The whole absorption complex (i.e. all five regions illustrated in Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig4"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig4" class="link xref-fig">4</a>) comprises a total of 62 velocity components for the LFC-calibrated spectrum (63 for the ThAr-calibrated). An initial trial fit showed that some badly-blended (and/or weak) velocity components provided only very poor constraints. In those cases we grouped components on small scales, resulting in a total of 47 individual measurements of ÎÎ±/Î±.</p><p class="chapter-para">The 47 ÎÎ±/Î± measurements obtained this way are shown in Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig6"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig6" class="link xref-fig">6</a> for both the LFC (top panel) and the ThAr (middle panel) models. The weighted average ÎÎ±/Î± across each of the five regions is tabulated in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl3" data-open="tbl3" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">3</a>, together with their statistical uncertainties. The results are in good agreement with the results obtained previously, i.e. the weighted average of ÎÎ±/Î± measurements within each region falls within 1Ï of the results in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a>.</p>                    <a id="210901138" scrollto-destination="210901138"></a>

the results obtained in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a>, the weighted average over the 47 measurements for the LFC-calibrated spectrum (ÎÎ±/Î±Â = â0.18Â Â±Â 2.11Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup>) and for the ThAr-calibrated spectrum (ÎÎ±/Î±Â = 0.88Â Â±Â 1.99Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup>) are consistent with each other. As expected, given the large number of free model parameters, there is generally a slight increase in the ÎÎ±/Î± error estimates (compare TablesÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl2"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl2" data-open="tbl2" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">2</a> andÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl3" data-open="tbl3" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">3</a>).</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901141 id="210901141" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3-5>3.5 Consistency between 47 Î± measurements</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">We now explore the differences between the LFC and ThAr models in more detail. We bin the individual ÎÎ±/Î± measurements in redshift bins Î<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span>Â = 1Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â4</sup> (the 47 measurements fall into 37 bins) and calculate the weighted average in each. Their differences (LFC minus ThAr) are illustrated in the lower panel of Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig6"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig6" class="link xref-fig">6</a>. The LFC and ThAr measurements agree well everywhere except in region I. Most of the LFC-ThAr differences in region I are located around â2Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â5</sup>, with two bins (at the high-redshift end) at more negative values (â1.8 and â7.6Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â4</sup>). The top two panels of Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig6"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig6" class="link xref-fig">6</a> suggest this is caused by velocity structure differences between the LFC and ThAr models. Discarding the points in these two bins and taking the weighted average of the remaining points in region I, we get â4.90Â Â±Â 5.49Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup> for LFC and 15.68Â Â±Â 8.54Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup> for ThAr, i.e. the two remain inconsistent.</p><p class="chapter-para">The most significant deviation from zero occurs in region II (â3Ï). Measurements from the LFC-calibrated and the ThAr-calibrated spectrum are in excellent agreement in this region.</p><p class="chapter-para">A substantial advantage of deriving ÎÎ±/Î± measurements for individual absorbing components (or small groups) is that it may help to identify and filter out any possible rogue measurements. The least trimmed squares (LTS) method is frequently used for this and provides a more robust estimate of the mean. We thus apply LTS here, discarding 15Â perâcent of the data in each region. The discarded components are listed in column 6 of TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl3" data-open="tbl3" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">3</a>. The weighted averages for each region and the entire sample after LTS trimming are tabulated in columns 7 and 8 of the same table. Interestingly, the consequence of removing the most outlying measurement in region II was to move the regionâs average towards more positive values (but doubling the error and therefore making the result less significant).</p><p class="chapter-para">The measured scatter (i.e. the empirical standard deviation) of the remaining 40 LFC-calibrated measurements is ÏÂ = 9Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â5</sup>. This is slightly smaller than the average error on individual measurements, â©Ï<sup><em>stat</em></sup>âªÂ = 14Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â5</sup>. The scatter of the ThAr-calibrated measurements is ÏÂ = 13Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â5</sup> (the same as the average error).</p>                    <h3 scrollto-destination=210901146 id="210901146" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec3-6>3.6 Consistency with previous studies</h3>
<p class="chapter-para">Recently, a detailed study of this same absorption complex was carried out (KotuÅ¡ etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib46"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib46" data-open="bib46" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>) using spectra from the UVES spectrograph (Dekker etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib27"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib27" data-open="bib27" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2000</a>) on the Very Large Telescope. Those spectra are higher S/N although the spectral resolution is lower. Comparing with those results reveals good consistency. In this paper we split the data into five distinct regions whereas KotuÅ¡ etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib46"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib46" data-open="bib46" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>) use three. However, combining our regions {I+II} (âleftâ) and {IIIÂ +Â IV} (âcentreâ) enables the comparison (our region V corresponds to KotuÅ¡â ârightâ region). Using the ÎÎ±/Î± results from the LTS trimmed sample in our TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl3" data-open="tbl3" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">3</a> (LFC-calibrated) and KotuÅ¡â table 4, and combining all random and systematic errors appropriately, the ÎÎ±/Î± solution differ by 1.15Ï, 1.02Ï, and 0.92Ï respectively, left to right.</p><p class="chapter-para">Prior to our study, KotuÅ¡ etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib46"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib46" data-open="bib46" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>) was the most detailed study. However, several prior analyses also exist (Quast etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib75"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib75" data-open="bib75" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2004</a>; Levshakov etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib50"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib50" data-open="bib50" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2005</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib51"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib51" data-open="bib51" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2006</a>; Chand etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib24"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib24" data-open="bib24" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2006</a>; Molaro etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib63"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib63" data-open="bib63" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2008</a>). All produced results consistent with no change in Î±, with somewhat larger uncertainties than derived from our analysis or that of KotuÅ¡ etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib46"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib46" data-open="bib46" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2017</a>).</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901149 id="210901149" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec4>4 RESULTS</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig7"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig7" class="link xref-fig">7</a> shows the ÎÎ±/Î± measurements tabulated in TablesÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl2"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl2" data-open="tbl2" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">2</a> andÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl3" data-open="tbl3" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">3</a>. The two large panels show the results from the LFC-calibrated (top panel) and the ThAr-calibrated spectrum (bottom panel). The weighted average for the entire <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span> â 1.15 absorption complex are plotted in the small panels to the right of the main panels. Our main results are summarized as follows:</p><ul class="list-simple"><li><p class="chapter-para">In the analysis presented in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a>, we obtain five ÎÎ±/Î± measurement (one per region) from the LFC-calibrated and from the ThAr-calibrated spectrum independently. For the LFC-calibrated spectrum, the average over the five regions is consistent with no variation in Î±. Applying the same methods to the ThAr calibrated spectrum, we obtain a 2.5Ï deviation from zero. These results are tabulated in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl2"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl2" data-open="tbl2" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">2</a> and plotted as black points in Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig7"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig7" class="link xref-fig">7</a>.</p></li><li><p class="chapter-para">Including ÎÎ±/Î± as a free parameter for 47 individual absorption components (or appropriately grouped components) allows us to identify regions of data significantly affecting the overall measurement (SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-4"></span><a href="#sec3-4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.4</a>). These results, tabulated in TableÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-tbl3"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="tbl3" data-open="tbl3" class="link link-reveal link-table xref-fig">3</a> and plotted as red squares on Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig7"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig7" class="link xref-fig">7</a>, are in excellent agreement with the results from SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a>.</p></li><li><p class="chapter-para">To explore robustness, we apply LTS, removing 15Â perâcent of the sample in each region, obtaining the results plotted as green diamonds in Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig7"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig7" class="link xref-fig">7</a>. This reduces the total number of measurements to 40. The weighted average over the 40 measurements is consistent with zero for both calibrations: ÎÎ±/Î±Â = â0.27Â Â±Â 2.41Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup> (LFC) and ÎÎ±/Î±Â = â0.15Â Â±Â 2.44Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup> (ThAr).</p></li><li><p class="chapter-para">When using the approach in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a>, the wavelength scale distortions imparted by the ThAr calibration methods (see Fig.Â <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-fig1"></span><a href="javascript:;" data-modal-source-id="fig1" class="link xref-fig">1</a>) have a small, but measurable, effect. The same distortions appear to have no effect on the measurements in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-4"></span><a href="#sec3-4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.4</a>.</p></li><li><p class="chapter-para">The HE0515â4414 absorption complex modelled spans approximately 700Â km s<sup>â1</sup>. If this system represents a line of sight through a cluster of order 1 Mpc across, we can place an upper limit, for the first time, on small-scale Î± variations, using the empirical scatter in the 40 ÎÎ±/Î± measurements. The upper limit on small-scale Î± variations over scale lengths â25Â kpc, is â9Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â5</sup> (SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-5"></span><a href="#sec3-5" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.5</a>).</p></li><li><p class="chapter-para">Averaged over all absorption components, we derive a gas temperature of <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$T=9.12\pm 1.08\times 10^3\, {\rm K}$|</span> (SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-2"></span><a href="#sec3-2" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.2</a>). This value is in agreement with the results from Carswell etÂ al. (<span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib23"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib23" data-open="bib23" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2012</a>) who found <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$T=12\pm 3\times 10^3\, {\rm K}$|</span> in a quasar absorption system at <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$z$|</span><sub>abs</sub>Â = 2.076. As seen, the new data presented in this paper provide a more stringent constraint and also suggest that all individual absorption components are consistent with a single gas temperature.</p></li></ul>                    <a id="210901152" scrollto-destination="210901152"></a>
<div data-id="fig7" data-content-id="fig7" class="fig fig-section js-fig-section" swap-content-for-modal="true"><div class="graphic-wrap"><img class="content-image" src="https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/mnras/500/1/10.1093_mnras_staa3217/2/m_staa3217fig7.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&amp;Signature=oGPW~-SMHBg9-u8L5SdU83dcR5anB4uUgiFz~MZ-0vWLeK-k4tZaVdFBSFZS~l3dtqJbJWTBpCf1VxjhrNsSQTz7wE7578UluFJOpt8Ko8U0Z5qWNmEnJZOSRU6tmrfZnh98c8oe9dfRPeROfhTeVMV68DsfwPEMCLAy7YrqxWzbZPlcoSkZi5ske1nIvqlIs~c25g4cDrjt3cVUW0LbP9O92dYx4jQQlZk7~to9aA0lbL-gmhB8Zxm~XtqnJJmHQ0-ivvlxJaskfK6E~b1Mp7nGKlWP9pkvwpOAQnTIjjFHeNdgCmdnc6DeNPNAQxPBCEQthoXiVe-w1HVn2ceptA__&amp;Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA" alt="Comparing the different approaches to measuring Î± from the LFC-calibrated spectrum (top) and the ThAr-calibrated spectrum (bottom). Panels on the right show the weighted average over the entire absorption complex." data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig7.jpg" /><div class="graphic-bottom"><div class="label fig-label" id="label-210901152">Figure 7.</div><div class="caption fig-caption"><p class="chapter-para">Comparing the different approaches to measuring Î± from the LFC-calibrated spectrum (top) and the ThAr-calibrated spectrum (bottom). Measurements from SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a> are shown as black points. The weighted average of the 47 measurements, derived in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-4"></span><a href="#sec3-4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.4</a>, are shown as red squares. The green diamonds are after outlier removal. Points were offset along the <em>x</em>-axis for clarity. Panels on the right show the weighted average over the entire absorption complex.</p></div><div class="ajax-articleAbstract-exclude-regex fig-orig original-slide figure-button-wrap"><a class="fig-view-orig js-view-large at-figureViewLarge openInAnotherWindow" role="button" aria-describedby="label-210901152" href="/view-large/figure/210901152/staa3217fig7.jpg" data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig7.jpg" target="_blank">Open in new tab</a><a class="download-slide" role="button" aria-describedby="label-210901152" data-section="210901152" href="/DownloadFile/DownloadImage.aspx?image=https://oup.silverchair-cdn.com/oup/backfile/Content_public/Journal/mnras/500/1/10.1093_mnras_staa3217/2/staa3217fig7.jpeg?Expires=2147483647&Signature=g7oxGYTnzEce7onhnplF4Z4H9SJ2EkzmTiIXbrlJiFsikxIygfFuwfYDJ1YeKNTjT3TDo-6-VzaSCo51jOox28j1Ad~KLB-wylgoSFlLQqWakhjs-18xljpm~RnnbqQuwpUz9SiK~NP5Zln4b35FDtm43Dd4BjqfAX2jHSNLi2F4Kz-vqdYVwHtp-5LpvasRAunSpJiRn6GgQV4ANlmILWy~ZgewYKOxmwA5YqWhfrSNd3ezjNL8afBSngCQMqgY~KIJnqlKEoD-YTHU1HP1FR2l-fl9eI-eLZKDgIAiutbCQqRlSiW3A0CWaJJqeHtoqBpmA4VzRTp-ufnikJSsPA__&Key-Pair-Id=APKAIE5G5CRDK6RD3PGA&sec=210901152&ar=5935255&xsltPath=~/UI/app/XSLT&imagename=&siteId=5326" data-path-from-xml="staa3217fig7.jpg">Download slide</a></div></div></div></div>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901153 id="210901153" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec5>5 DISCUSSION</h2>
<p class="chapter-para">In this work we have analysed the first LFC-calibrated quasar spectrum. Quasar spectra of similar quality to the one presented here will be routinely produced by the new ESPRESSO spectrograph installed on the Very Large Telescope (Pepe etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib70"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib70" data-open="bib70" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2014</a>) and by the future HIRES instrument on the Extremely Large Telescope (Maiolino etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib56"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib56" data-open="bib56" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2013</a>). Both of these instruments have LFCs for wavelength calibration. We hope that the new methods introduced in this paper will be beneficial in analysing future observations.</p><p class="chapter-para">We have demonstrated that careful modelling procedures play a crucial part in the analysis of high-resolution spectroscopic data. Tools such as <span class="monospace">AI-VPFIT</span>Â eliminate any potential human bias and yield optimal models of the data in a reproducible and objective manner.</p><p class="chapter-para">Choosing the correct line broadening mechanism, i.e. including temperature parameters for individual components, is important. Models produced assuming an incorrect broadening mechanism tend to generate artificial close blends of absorption lines. Modelling simulated data, based on the HE0515 spectrum used in this work, shows that using an incorrect broadening mechanism biases Î± measurements (Lee etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib49"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib49" data-open="bib49" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2020</a>).</p><p class="chapter-para">Examining the scatter of the five measurements obtained from the LFC-calibrated spectrum in SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-3"></span><a href="#sec3-3" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.3</a>, we find they have Ï<sup>2</sup>Â Â = 21.99 (<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">â |$\nu$|</span>Â = 4) . For a Ï<sup>2</sup> distribution with four degrees of freedom, the probability of observing Ï<sup>2</sup> values at least this large is <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$p=0.02{{\ \rm per\ cent}}$|â </span>. The five measurements are therefore highly inconsistent with each other. Performing the same for the 47 measurements from SectionÂ <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-sec3-4"></span><a href="#sec3-4" class="sectionLink xref-sec js-xref-sec">3.4</a>, we find the LFC-calibrated measurements have a Ï<sup>2</sup>Â = 72.86 (<span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">â |$\nu$|</span>Â = 46, <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$p=0.7{{\ \rm per\ cent}}$|â </span>). We assume this is not caused by small-scale spatial variations in Î± across the redshift range covered by the absorption system. After LTS trimming, the scatter in the remaining 40 measurements are consistent with their individually estimated errors (Ï<sup>2</sup>Â = 23.76, <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$\nu$|</span>Â = 39, <span class="inline-formula no-formula-id">|$p=97{{\ \rm per\ cent}}$|â </span>). Similar results are obtained for the ThAr spectrum.</p><p class="chapter-para">The analysis presented here is based on the assumption of solar relative isotopic abundances. Significant deviations from solar values translate to large shifts in ÎÎ±/Î± (Webb etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib98"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib98" data-open="bib98" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">1999</a>; Ashenfelter, MathewsÂ &amp; Olive <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib5"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib5" data-open="bib5" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2004a</a>, <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib6"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib6" data-open="bib6" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">b</a>; Fenner, MurphyÂ &amp; Gibson <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib36"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib36" data-open="bib36" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2005</a>; Berengut, KavaÂ &amp; Flambaum <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib17"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib17" data-open="bib17" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2012</a>; Webb etÂ al. <span class="xrefLink" id="jumplink-bib100"></span><a href="javascript:;" reveal-id="bib100" data-open="bib100" class="link link-ref link-reveal xref-bibr">2014</a>). Very approximately, when simultaneously modelling Mgâ<span class="small-caps">ii</span> and Feâ<span class="small-caps">ii</span>, the measured ÎÎ±/Î± may shift towards negative values by as much as â5Â ÃÂ 10<sup>â6</sup> for 100Â perâcent <sup>24</sup>Mg and by the same amount in the positive direction for 100Â perâcent <sup>25+26</sup>Mg. A discussion as to the validity of the solar isotopic assumption is deferred to a subsequent paper.</p><p class="chapter-para">For these particular observations, LFC calibration methods have not yielded significantly different ÎÎ±/Î± measurements compared to the ThAr methods. The probable reason for this lies in the fact that we have combined a large number of individual exposures to form a final co-added spectrum. Due to different barycentric velocities for each observation, the position of relevant transitions falls differently with respect to the complicated distortion pattern each time, effectively smearing it out. This is less likely to occur for more efficient spectrographs, such as ESPRESSO and HIRES.</p>                    <h2 scrollto-destination=210901160 id="210901160" class="section-title js-splitscreen-section-title" data-legacy-id=sec1-07>SUPPORTING INFORMATION</h2>
